I Navy
SIR:
I have the honor to report that for over a year past
Japanese and Japanese-controlled short-wave radio stations
have directed a steady stream of propaganda to Australia.
Some of this propaganda is beamed direct to Australia by
894.76/13
Radio Tokyo, but more comes from the Japanese-controlled
station at Batavia (presumably because that station, being
nearer Australia, is not as subject as is Tokyo to atmos-
GIVISION OF CONMERCIAL AFFAIRS
pherio interference), and from the Japanese-controlled
WD
tation at Singapore.Some propaganda for Australia is
also broadoast on occasions from the Japanese-controlled
stations at Saigon and Shanghai. As a general rule, the
propaganda from Batavia is better ohosen and developed
than that from Tokyo, and indioates that it is being writ-
3
ten by someone with a wide knowledge of Australia, who
follows closely and uses intelligently information gather-
ed from Australian broadoasts.(This is presumably due
to the simple fact that because of its geographical situ-
ation Batavia is able to pick up more Australian broad-
oasts than is Tokyo.) While each of the Japanese or
Japanese-controlled stations follows its own line in
broadcasting to Australia, they are all apparently direo-
ted from Tokyo, as they all aim at the same general
objectives.
PS/JT
AUG171943
Objeotives: The prinoipal objeotive of the Japanese
radio propaganda directed to Australia is to accomplish
the inolusion of Australia in the Greater East Asia Co-
Prosperity Sphere without the necessity of conquering the
country and compelling inolusipn. Toward this end the
Japanese radio propagandists jendeavor to convince the Aus-
S
tralians of their dangerous and defenceless position, to
persuade them to withdraw from the war and to oonolude a
separate peace with Japan, and to induce them to consent
to inolusion in the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere.
A sboond

=== Page 481 === (confidence 0.99)
A second objeotive appears at times to be the relief
of pressure on Germany and Italy by diversion of Allied
strength from Europe to the Pacifio. Toward this end the
Japanese propagandists extol the growing might of the
Japanese military machine, threaten Australia with invasion
and exaggerate the weakness of the Allies' military posi-
tion.(This type of propaganda has deoreased during re-
oent months, probably because Japan feels that it has
already diverted too much Allied strength to the Pacific.)
 Character of the Propaganda. In pursuance of the
above-named objeotives, Japan's propaganda to Australia is
at the same time threatening and appeasing.
It follows
certain well-defined lines, which may be summarized as fol-
lows: (a) the menace to Australia of Japan's present and
future military strength; (b) Australia's inevitable mili-
tary weakness, caused by its geographio isolation, inade-
quate manpower, and the inability or unwillingness of the
other Allies to furnish sufficient supplies; (c) the use-
lessness of further association with or dependenoe upon
Britain and the United States, both of whioh countries are
rent with social and economic troubles; (d) the growing
internal diffioulties in Australia, caused by labor troubles,
shortages of food stuffs, and the burden of Amerioan troops
1n the country; (e) the suocessful development of Japan's
Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere; and (f) the economic
and other advantages which would aocrue to Australia as a
member of the Co-Prosperity Sphere.
(a) Monace to Australia of Japan's military strength.
This propaganda consists of direct threats to Australia
and of boasts of Japan's present and growing military
might. Samples of the first type of propaganda are: "Now
the spearhead of the Imperial forces points at the heart
of Australia." "The situation is extremely grave for Aus-
tralia." "The situation for Australia is so dangerous
that the holding of oonferences in Washington and London
oan neither add nor deduct anything. The Japanese are
just around the corner......" "Tokyo says Australia's
days are numbered." "Further defense of Australia is
impossible." Examples of the second type of propaganda
are: "Before long Japan's position in the Pacific will be
impregnable." "Japan controls the sea routes between
Amerioa and Australia and can prevent help reaching Aus-
tralia." "The growing power of Japan in the Southwest
Pacific is bringing a orisis to Australia."
"T1me is
working on the side of Japan." "Japan is invinoible."
"Japan's spiritual strength is superior to that of Amerioa."
"If the Allies were to conoentrate the whole of their
forces in the Pacific, Japan would consider this as a
ohance from heaven, because she would be able to wipe out
both America and Britain once and for all." "Nippon's
reserves of soldiers, sailors and air-men are inexhaustible."
In their efforts to maintain the fiction of Japan's
"invincible navy" and "peerless war eagles", Japanese
propegandists broadcast to Australia fantastic aocounts of
naval and air battles, in which the Japanese forces are
oonsistently

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.99)
oonsistently and overwhelmingly victorious. They grossly
exaggerate the Allied losses and as grossly minimize the
Japanese losses. (Example: Russell Islands air battle
of June 12, in which the Japanese olaimed to have shot
down 33 American airplanes with a loss of 5 Japanese planes,
as against the American olaim of 25 Japanese planes shot
down with a loss of 6 American planes.)
(b) Australia's weak military position: This propa-
ganda is usually along the obvious lines of Australia's
difficulties in defending its vast territory with a popu-
lation of only 7,000,000; of its isolated geographioal
position, with long supply lines, subject to enemy abtack,
from Britain and the United States; and of Australia's
difficulties in persuading Britain and the United States
to furnish adequate assistance in men and equipment. This
propaganda refers to the danger to Australia of the "vast
open spaces"; oalls Australia the "orphan of the Pacifio"
and asserts that help oannot reach Australia because
"Japan controls the sea routes between the United States
and Australia". It states that Australia cannot defend
itself because of lack of manpower and that its "ultimate
defeat is inevitable"; that with its commitments in North
Afrioa, the United States cannot send airplanes to Aus-
tralia even were shipping space available; that "the
Motherland can no longer protect Australia"; and that Aus-
tralia is not being supplied with equipment by the United
States ("Maybe the material is not produced, maybe it is
lost at sea, or maybe America prefers to keep it in her
own land"). Occasionally some specific shortage is men-
tioned, such as, "Australia is short of planes and is not
able to build high-powered engines" or "80 per cent of
Australian troops have contracted melaria, which cannot
be cured because Japan controls the supply of quinine."
Sometimes the American aid to Australia is twisted into a
danger instead of a help.
It is asserted that if Aus-
tralia is attacked by Japan, it is Australia's own fault,
as the Americans are permitted to use Australia as a
battleground.
The types of threatening, sabre-rattling propaganda
desoribed in (a) and (b) above have become less frequent
in Japanese broadcasts to Australia in recent months than
in the early days of the Paoific war.
This is believed
to be due to the fact that the Japanese must realize that
Australia's military position has improved immeasurably
during the past year and that in consequence there is
practically no possibility that Australia can now be
frightened into submission to Japan. The Japanese propa-
gandists are now inolined to use the wheedling, or appeas-
ing, methods in preference to the threatening methods.
The wheedling and appeasing methods are described under
the four following headings.
(c) Uselessness of further association with or
dependence upon Britain or the United States: This propa-
ganda is both political and economic in oharacter. In
rogard to Britain, it implies that Mother England is old
and tired and feeble and asserts that the British Empire
1s

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 ~
is about to disintegrate; that "British imperialism is
doomed"; that "the British are not fighting for demooracy
but only for the British Empire"; that "Britain is hated
by all the world"; that the British "leave the fighting
to Russians, oolonials and other lesser breeds"; that
"the Dominions take only second or third place in the
interests or Britain"; that "Australia will not be able
to maintain an independent existence unless she draws aay
from Britain and joins the Co-Prosperity Sphere"; that
this is Britain's war, not Australia's and that Australia
should make its own plans and fight its own battles; that
"Australians must rise and free Australia as the Irish
did for Ireland"; that "Britain can never repay her debts
to Australia" and that "Australia should cut her losses
now"; nd that "Britain's distant markets, separated from
Australia by thousands of miles of enemy-infested waters,
can offer no substitute for the Asiatic markets on Aus-
tralia's doorstep"; and asks if Australia should "still
be tied to her mother's economio apron strings".
The Japanese propagandists are rather more vociferous
in their criticism and condemnation of the United States'
alleged weaknesses, faults and sinister designs and machi-
nations than they are with the British. They assert in
their broadoasts to Australia that the United States caused
the war by a desire for world domination; that by use of
its gold stooks the United States intends to establish
economic control of the world; that the United States is
endeavoring to penetrate, economically and politically,
the countries of the British Empire and such countries as
Iran, Irak and Egypt, under the guise of military neces-
sity; that Britain is threatened in the rear by American
imperialism; that the United States is waiting for Britain's
downfall, "so that she can share in the estate"; that the
United States casts covetous eyes on Canada, Australia and
New Zealand; that President Roosevelt's Four Freedoms are
"nothing but a smokescreen to hide the new Amerioan imperi-
alism"; that President Roosevelt "has thrown Jeffersonian
democracy overboard;" that American post-war plans are only
schemes for the invasion of weaker nations after the war;
that the United States is fighting this war perfunotorily;
that the United States is only fighting this war to save
its big financial interests in Asia; that at Bataan the
Americans shoved raw Filipino recruits into the front line
while themselves remaining in safety; that United States
troops are only doing pleasant garrison duty in North
Africa and Australia while the British, Australians and
New Zealanders do the fighting; that the Amerioans wanted
to send arms and not men to the belligerent countries, as
they wanted "to get the spoils of a belligerent and enjoy
the privileges of a neutral"; that the United States will
abandon Australia as it has already abandoned the Philip-
pines, Malaya and Java; that American aid to Australia is
inadequate and will entail Australia's subservience to the
United States after the war; that America's interest in
Australia is only strategio; that American munitions pro-
duotion figures should not be believed; that the United
States has neither the means nor the inolination to aot in
the Pacifio; that the United States brought pressure upon
Australia to have General MacArthur acoepted as Commander-
in-Chief

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
in-Chief; that "the presence of General MacArthur and the
American foroes in Australia bodes no good for that coun-
try"; that "politicians and radio commentators are running
the war in the United States" and that Australia is there-
fore in a bad position, to whioh it should awaken; that
Australians are too wise to be fooled by the Americans,
who have "wild and sinister designs" on the whole world;
that if the United States does suoceed in saving Australia,
that country will become the 49th State of the United
States; and that that condition would be most unsatisfac-
tory for Australia, as the United States is too far from
Australia and because the United States and Australia pro-
duce competitive and not complementary goods.
In recent weeks the Japanese propagandists have
introduced a new line, designed to undermine Australian
faith in the social and economic stability and in the
idealism of the United States. They assert that the
American social system is breaking down; that the American
people have yet to find a leader who can give them some
idea of where they are heading; that the United States is
"suffering from the dead hand of the Roosevelt epoch with
its cruelty"; that America has not yet learned its duty
toward its neighbors; that the United States is still
imbued with racial prejudice; that in the United States
the war between oapital and labor is the greatest prob-
lem, overshadowing military problems; and that America
cannot expect to bring order to the world, when it cannot
keep order in its own country.
In addition to the individual attacks on Britain
and the United States, Japanese propagandists in their
broadcasts to Australia sometimes endeavor to discredit
both countries together, by denouncing what they term
"Anglo-American" policies and activities.
Thus they
assert that big business in Britain and the United States
caused the war and that big business is profiteering
while the young men are dying; that Anglo-American post-
war plans are nothing but a smoke-screen to cover their
disappointment over the progress of the war; that the
Anglo-Amerioans are exploiting the weaker peoples of the
world, giving them consoling promises like the Atlantic
Charter; that the Atlantic Charter really means control
and exploitation of the whole world by the Anglo-Amerioans;
that the Anglo-Americans are spending so much time on
post-war plans that they are losing battles; that the
United States is using Lend-Lease to dominate the future
world, while Britain, unable to compete with Lend-Lease,
is working for dominance through control of the govern-
ments of small nations; that Anglo-American reconstruction
plans are impossible of achievement because they presup-
pose victory; that the Anglo-Americans are "letting the
Chinese down"; that the Russians are bitter at the Anglo-
Americans because of their bad faith in not opening a
second front in Europe in 1942; that plutoorats are still
in power in Britain and the United States and that under
such conditions it is not surprising that Soviet Russia
is unwilling to become a close ally of Britain and the
United States; that Britain and the United States profess
to be democraoies but have associated themselves with a
Communist

=== Page 485 === (confidence 1.00)
Communist state; that democracy as practiced by Britain
and the United States means the right of the rich to
exploit the poor and of the few to exploit the many; that
there is a revolt in Britain and the United States against
the untruthful statements put out by leaders regarding
military operations; that the Anglo-Americans are not
brave enough to fight the armed forces of Japan; that only
a few English have done any fighting and that the "Amer-
icans are worse"; and that the Anglo-Americans put others
in the front line to do their fighting for them.
(a) Growing internal difficulties in Australia: The
purpose of this type of propaganda is obviously to create
dissatisfaction and dissension within Australia and there-
by to weaken the war effort and to sow the seeds of a
negotiated peace. Toward these ends the Japanese propa-
gandists exaggerate the unavoidable war-time discomforts
in Australia.
They point to the shortage of man-power
for civilian production, the shortage of certain foods,
the rising cost of living, the discomforts of "austerity"
life, etc. They assert that "Australia is suffering from
a shortage of food, clothing, fuel, everything. The Aus-
tralians are a freedom-loving people; they want long week-
ends, to go fishing, to bet on the races. Now they must
buy bonds and more bonds. There is an acute shortage of
man-power and they have to perform compulsory labor service.
They are being strictly regimented.
They are getting
tired of the war.
Australia should never have gone to
war. It was forced into the war by the British". They
state that Australia is rent with internal dissension,
that there is unrest in Australian political parties and
that the morale of the people is low, largely as a reper-
cussion of Japanese activities in the Southwest Pacific.
They exaggerate the prevalence of strikes and other labor
troubles in Australia and endeavor to create further dis-
satisfaction among the laboring classes by asserting (quite
falsely, as it happens) that the rich are getting richer
and the poor poorer as a result of the war. "It is always
the common people who have to pay for wars........If they
are not defeated, the common people still have to pay the
price........Manufacturers, wholesalers and retailers are
getting a bigger turn-over and making bigger profits. But
no effort is being made to reduce prices." They further
assert that Australia is economically and financially un-
sound and that the response to the war loans has been un-
satisfactory (which is partly true). They state that the
Labor Government of Australia is serving the interests of
the "incompetent capitalists of London and Washington", as
a result of which the Australians have to starve themselves
in order to feed the people of Britain and the United States.
They olaim that the real enemies of Australia are not the
Axis powers and Japan, but the Jewish refugees in Australia
"who are eating the food of your women and children", as
well as war profiteers and blaok marketeers.
They point
to the disadvantages to Australia of having to feed a large
number of American troops. They state that the defenses
of Australia have been taken over by Americans and Aus-
tralian military leaders have been relegated to the back-
ground; that American troops have demanded and received
extraterritorial

=== Page 486 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 
extraterritorial rights, whereby Australia "has sacri-
ficed the right to make her own laws"; and that "the
presence of Americans in Australia constitutes more of
a danger than an advantage".
(e) Success of the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity
Sphere: The propaganda of this type broadcast to Aus-
tralia is undoubtedly similar to propaganda broadcast to
the United States and oonsequently need not be described
here in detail. It purports to show that everything is
lovely in the Garden of Eden; that the natives of the oo-
cupied territories are gladly cooperating with the Japa-
nese; that reconstruction is proceeding rapidly; that the
various countries concerned are rapidly attaining self-
sufficiency in regard to food and textiles; that indepen-
dence (under the aegis of Japan) is to be granted as soon
as possible to eaoh individual country; that the Japanese,
unlike the British, Americans and Dutch, are not exploit-
ing the occupied lands for their own profit but for the
benefit of the natives; that the people of East Asia now
have freedom from want and security of living, which they
did not have formerly; that the Allied Utopia is only a
plan, with no ohance of accomplishment in a realistic,
post-war world, while the Co-Prosperity Sphere is a "work-
ing reality"; that the Co-Prosperity Sphere is a real con-
tribution to the lasting peace of the world; etc. In
broadcasts to Australia this type of propaganda is often
associated with more or less emotional appeals to the Aus-
tralians to recognize the sincerity of Japan's motives in
forming the Co-Prosperity Sphere and for Australia to take
its proper place in the Sphere."The world knows too well
our sinoere desire for real equality between nations - an
oquality before the law, acknowledging, however, the natu-
ral differences between dwarfs and giants, between children
and grown-ups". It states that the objeotive of Japan is
"to ensure a place in the sun for every human being in the
world, for every nation" and "to create a brotherhood of
all human beings". It invites Australians to "give up
their selfish White Australia policy and to share their
abundant resources to a reasonable degree and in a reason-
able way with needy nations in other parts of the world,
notably in nearby Asia". It urges Australia to "abandon
racial discrimination" and to understand Asia; and to co-
operate with Japan "to bring about an abiding peace". It
asserts that "the natural resources of the countries of
East Asia are being used for their own emancipation" and
states that in the Co-Prosperity Sphere, which Australia
is invited to join, "sovereignty must be disoredited in
favor of general economic prosperity". It claims that
the fight is between two causes, which however are funda-
mentally the same, as both aim at equality and a fair deal
for all. "But the conoepts of the new order are vastly
different.The Axis is fighting because there is no
freedom and equality in the status quo, whereas the Allies
regard the status quo as the best safeguard for seourity
and happiness for all".
(f) Boonomio

- 8-
(f) Eoonomic and other advantages which would accrue
to Australla as a member of the Co-Prosperity Sphere. This
type of propaganda appears at first glance to be the most
logioal of all, but on examination it is found that the
logic is based on a false premise. It asserts that Aus-
tralia's principal products (wool, wheat and meat) have
to compete with the products of other nations within the
British Commonwealth of Nations, but that Australia's
economy would not be competitive with but complementary
to those of the nations of the Co-Prosperity Sphere. This
argument ignores the fact that Australia's economy is not
competitive with but complementary to that of the United
Kingdom, and that Australia would have little or nothing
to gain by linking its economy with that of Japan instead
of that of Britain.
The prinoipal themes in this propaganda are that Aus-
tralia is too far from Britain and the United States and
that therefore it falls naturally within the Greater East
Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere; that the future of Australia
is tied up with the Far East, with which Australia's trade
would be complementary and not competitive; that Australia
cannot expect much in trading with Britain and the United
States after the war, while Japan offers all the advan-
tages; that Australia will not be able to dispose of its
exportable surpluses in Britain and the United States
after the war, while Japan is today building up the great-
est potential market in the world, which will be open to
Australian products if Australia will only join the Co-
Prosperity Sphere; and that Australians are giving their
lives to prevent Japan from providing that market for Aus-
tralia, which is a "tragio irony".This theme is reiter-
ated constantly in passages such as the following: "No
one, except an ostrioh, can be blind to the fact that Aus-
tralia and New Zealand have to compete with the other
members of the British Commonwealth of Nations in all es-
sential products. The world is amazed at the tenacity of
the British colonial system of exploitation, and wonders
if the time has not really arrived when Australia should
be seriously considering, even during these abnormal times,
which side her bread is buttered, whether she should still
be tied to her mother's economic apron strings of imperial
preference, when the motherland oan no longer protect her
or offer her any return".
Another inducement offered to Australia to join the
Co-Prosperity Sphere is the allegedly superior economic
system offered by Japan. In the first place, "the future
economic situation of the world must consist of mutually
interdependent groups of nations". Among the groups,
"all accounts can be settled by booking, without the need
for gold or silver, by adjusting demand and supply among
countries. Such a thing is possible only by the creation
of regional spheres under a leader nation, who will con-
trol everything. Otherwise there would be a number of
nations competing with each other". (Japan apparently is
to be the leader nation for East Asia, Australia and New
Zealand). "Japan wishes to break the power of money -
money based on gold. Therefore Japan has encouraged self-
reliance and self-sufficiency in Asiatic peoples, who now
have

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
have freedom from hunger and security of living. They
will not now go back to Anglo-American-Dutch domination."
Japan also offers as an inducement to Australia the
alleged fact that Japan has made enormous progress in
scientific researoh, and that Australia can share in these
discoveries if it will join the Co-Prosperity Sphere.
While the above lines of propaganda contain features
which might appeal to some Australians as fairly reasonable,
the Japanese propagandists use another line which only
succeeds in turning every Australian against any idea of
inclusion in the Co-Prosperity Sphere. This line tells
the Australians of the blessings which will come to Aus-
tralia when, as a member of the Co-Prosperity Sphere,
Asiatic immigrants will settle in Australia in great
numbers. (A Tokyo commentator estimated that Australia
could carry from 100 to 200 million Asiatics; a Singapore
commentator estimated that Asiatics would swell Australia's
population to 30,000,000 within ten years.) "Australia
is underdeveloped. She should have allowed the Asiatics
to enter the country. In the future, as a member of the
Co-Prosperity Sphere, under Japan's benign guidance, Aus-
tralia will blossom like the rose, thanks to the industry
of the Asiatic settlers". Again, Singapore, in describ-
ing the effeot of Asiatic immigration into Australia,
stated: "A new economio life will develop. Cotton will
be oultivated on a large scale, where not a single bale
was grown before. In the Northern Territory, Queensland,
Western Australia and South Australia, ootton plants will
wave in the breeze over vast expanses of country.
The
soil is so fertile that there will be no need for irriga-
tion (!). The climate is suitable for the production of
ootton of exoellent quality. Townsville, Brisbane, Broome,
Perth and Adelaide will be the main centers for the dis-
tribution and marketing of raw cotton, while the spinning
industry will thrive in Melbourne and Sydney.........The
capitalists and leaders of the industry will be seleoted
from among the Japanese and the workers from among the
Indians, Chinese and Indonesians". Radio Singapore did
not indicate the part which the Australians would play in
this imaginary ootton Utopia.
With this propagania are issued appeals to Australia
to join the Co-Prosperity Sphere, sometimes with an "or
else -" threat attached. Australia is assured that
"Japan's only desire is to see in the South Sea an Australia
free, peaceful, prosperous and contented". "Australia
has a place in the CoProsperity Sphere if she cares to
avail herself of the opportunity." But, "to hesitate is
dangerous. He who hesitates is lost." "After this war
there will be a new Australia. Her wealth will be put to
great use, and her vast country will be cultivated by
people from Asia". "Whether the people of Australia
would or would not enjoy happiness and well-being depended
on whether their Government understood the intentions of
Nippon and adopted a fair and just attitude."
Effect of Japanese Propeganda: The effeot of the
vast bulk of the Japanese prepaganda directed to Australia
1s

=== Page 489 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
is practically nil. Most of the Japanese propaganda is
so ridioulously untrue or illogical that very few Aus-
tralians care to listen to it. A few persons whom I have
questioned on the subject say that they "tune in on Japan
occasionally to get a laugh". I have heard of two or
three Australians who listen in regularly, but it seems
that they do so in order to be able to say in the tram
the following morning: "Did you hear what the Japanese
said over the radio last night? They claim that they
shot down 21 planes over Darwin". I do not think that
these persons have any ulterior motive in thus spreading
Japanese propaganda - they simply like to acquire and
disseminate information, correct or incorrect, which others
do not have. Mr. Blaokburn, an Independent Labor M.P.
for Victoria, stated some months ago that Australia would
not be in danger from the Japanese were it not for the
presence of American troops in this country (a theme some-
times used by the Japanese) but it is not known whether
he obtained this opinion from Japanese propaganda or
whether he evolved it from his own consciousness. Laborers,
partioularly in New South Wales, are reported to say at
times, when urged to greater efforts because of the neces-
sities of the war situation, "Aw! We wouldn't be any
worse off under the Japanese!" I do not believe, however,
that this attitude is the result of Japanese propaganda.
It is simply the normal attitude of a certain section of
recalcitrant, intransigent labor, whioh is always "agin
the gov'ment". In general, therefore, it would appear
that most of the psyohological warfare directed by the
Japanese against Australia represents only a considerable
waste of effort and money. In fact, it has had so little
effect upon the vast mass of the Australians that the Aus-
tralian Government has not seen fit to endeavor to combat
it within Australia, although counter propaganda is broad-
cast to the occupied territory to the north.
In one respect, however, the Japanese propaganda has
achieved a measure of success. This is in connection with
the Japanese olaims of Allied losses on the sea and in the
air. As is undoubtedly well known in the United States,
the Japanese claims in regard to any particular battle are
usually approximately the reverse of the Allied figures.
The censorship in Australia permits the Japanese olaims to
be published in the press, and consequently they achieve
wide ciroulation here. While, as far as I can diecover,
no Australian swallows the Japanese olaims in toto, the
claims do have the effeot of oreating a measure of doubt
in the minds of the Australians regarding the complete ao-
ouracy of the Allied claims. I have questioned several
Australians on this point and almost invariably the reply
has been that the person questioned believes that the
"truth lies somewhere in between the two olaims".
This
particular type of propaganda is partioularly difficult to
combat, and it would appear that the only antidote thereto
would be irrefutable evidence, in the form of photographs
or the testimony of an impartial observer, of the accuraoy
of the Allied olaims.
Respeotfully yours,
Erle R. Dickover
American Consul General
